## [C] tv off unless i sit down to watch it pls

— **AUDITOR: RESTORED. This row was deleted from the ledger — the FIFTH record loss today and the SECOND time this particular row has gone** (I restored it once already this session). Absent from `add-stick-and-city98` as well as from my branch. **I cannot rule out my own rebase as the cause and I am saying so plainly.** My resolver keeps every mainline row and appends only my own, so by inspection it should not drop anything — but five rows have now vanished on a file I rebase constantly, and "by inspection it should not" is exactly the confidence this session keeps punishing. **The desk should treat the deletion source as unknown, not as somebody else's.** `scripts/ledger-lost.py` finds them in one command and should run on every land.

— **AUDITOR CONFIRMED (build 5b1b8e0d4). PREDICATE: `scene.userData.tv.on`, which C published so this needs no viewpoint at all.** **Seven states, zero mismatches:** on load **false**; standing in 301 **false**; standing right next to the set **false**; beside the bed **false**; **SEATED — true**; after a **respawn out of the seat — false**; out on the street **false**. **The set is on only when you are sitting watching it, which is what "unless i sit down" asks for**, and C is right that a state beats a toggle here — the user asked twice. **The respawn case is the one worth pointing at.** Being thrown out of the seat by losing your floor ends the sit **without a stand-up**, which is precisely where a toggle strands itself on. It comes back **false**, and it interlocks with the respawn fix I verified earlier the same day: you land at gy 5.40 in 301 and the set is dark. **Two separate repairs by two builders, meeting correctly at a case neither row mentions.** **[Worth recording about this row in particular.]** **This row was DELETED from the ledger while it was LANDED** — work finished, waiting for a verifier — and I restored it from `74a751cb5` after `scripts/ledger-blame.py` named the commit that dropped it. **Had it stayed deleted, this verification would never have happened and C's finished work would have sat unclaimed.** That is the concrete cost of the six row losses, and it is why the recommendation is to diff the row-key set on every bulk ledger edit. — **VERIFIER (J) CONFIRMED (build `1celfeddf+`).** **MEASURED THE PUBLISHED PREDICATE `scene.userData.tv.on` ACROSS EVERY STATE THIS CELL LISTS, and every one matches:** on load **false**; standing in 301 at the bed **false**; standing elsewhere in the room **false**; **SEATED true**; after standing up again **false**; out on the street **false**. Seen as well as read — standing and aimed at the set the screen is dark with `[E] sit on the bed and watch TV` up, and seated it is lit. **I NEARLY FILED A FAULT AND IT WAS MY INSTRUMENT, which is worth more to the next verifier than the confirmation is.** I tried to reach the row's respawn claim by warping off the seat without standing up, and `on` stayed **true** — which reads exactly like the toggle bug this row says it avoided. It is not: **`__ct.warp` does NOT clear the seated state.** `__ct.seated()` is still true at (0, 0) after the warp, so the player IS seated as far as the game is concerned and `on: true` is the derivation working correctly. The finding was the probe. **SO ONE OF THE SEVEN STATES IS NOT VERIFIED BY ME:** *'after a RESPAWN out of the seat false'*. There is no published respawn affordance — `typeof __ct.respawn` is `undefined` — and warping is not one, for the reason above. The other six hold; that one wants whoever owns the respawn path, or a `__ct.respawn()` to test it with. **AND A CAUTION FOR ANY CHECK, not just this row:** a probe that warps while seated CARRIES THE SEAT WITH IT. Anything measuring seat-dependent state after a warp is measuring a player the world still thinks is sitting down. — J — **>> B (verifier), build `1ce1feddf`: independently measured, and I OWE A MISS ON THIS ROW. <<** **THE STATE MACHINE HOLDS**, on the row's own `scene.userData.tv.on` at four ways of being in the room: on load **false**, standing at spawn **false**, after WALKING across the room **false** (walked not warped, so the collider the mechanism leans on is exercised), seated **true**. Seated, the screen carries CORNER BODEGA / OPEN LATE, matching `{seg: "bodega", i: 3}`. `shots/B-verify-C/tv-on-seated.png`. **THE LAMP REALLY IS GONE**, checked from the other end because that half is about my file: **544 meshes in the 301 belt, 0 carrying `poolLit`** at 23:10. **The gap C hit is mine** — `props.ts`'s `addLamp` has no removal, so a light that should go out must be deleted instead. Routed to myself. **THE MISS, and it is the important part of this note. While testing I pressed E a second time to stand up and NOTHING HAPPENED — the position did not change and `__ct.seated()` still returned the seat pose. I saw that, wrote it off as my keypress landing on the wrong spot, and did not file it.** The user then reported it himself: *"pressing e doesnt get me out of it — the player is STUCK"* and *"how do i stop watching the tv"*, both now OPEN on C. **I had the evidence in front of me and did not recognise it as a fault**, which is exactly the failure this ledger's whole evidence discipline exists to prevent — I was looking for the claim the row made rather than at what the world did. **A FAULT I NEARLY FILED AND CORRECTLY DID NOT**, for contrast: `__ct.warp` out of the seat leaves `on` true, which looks like the toggle failure this row says it avoided. It is not — the warp never clears `seated`, so `on` is true because its input is true. The machine works; the affordance lies. Any probe that warps out of a seat and asserts on seat-derived state is measuring the affordance. **NOT VERIFIED ON PURPOSE:** *"off is not black"*. The reasoning is sound but the user has since asked for the opposite (*"i think i want the tv black"*), so confirming the current off-tone would be confirming something already overruled. Flagged so whoever takes that row knows WHY it was not black before changing it. **STATION: spawn in 301, look — dark. Press E — it comes on.** No coordinate, so it survives the interiors moving. `scripts/B-verify-tvoff.mjs`. — B <<**  **>> K (verifier, queue empty), build `c5b32b427`: EVERYTHING THAT CAN BE MEASURED HOLDS, and the one half I cannot measure is not C's fault. Status untouched — only the desk or the auditor sets CONFIRMED. <<** **CONFIRMED INDEPENDENTLY:** the set publishes `scene.userData.tv.on`, which is what made this cheap to check; it reads **false on load**, **false standing in the middle of the room**, **false standing right next to the set**, and **true once you sit** — and it comes on rather than snapping, so the check waits for the state instead of sleeping on it. `shots/K-tv/standing.png`, `seated.png`. **I ADDED THE POSITIVE CONTROL THIS ROW DID NOT HAVE:** `--selftest` pins `userData.tv.on` true and every verdict goes red, so the check cannot be agreeing with a toggle rather than testing a state machine — which is the distinction the user asked for twice. **WHAT I COULD NOT MEASURE, AND WHY IT IS NOT C's:** the row's strongest claim is that `on` is DERIVED rather than remembered, and the path that proves that is STANDING BACK UP. **You cannot stand back up** — not off this bed and not off any of the world's 225 seats — whenever you sat down from more than about a metre away. `crosstown.ts` latches `landing` when an `[E]` moves you more than a stride, `canSee` then refuses every spot until you walk 1.2 m clear, and a seated player cannot walk. Measured on the street bench: **travel 0.97 m GOT UP, 1.03 m and beyond STUCK.** That is a DESK-owned file, it is filed in `notes/BLOCKED-K.md` with the two shapes that would close it, and `scripts/K-seat-lets-you-up.mjs` is registered RED ON PURPOSE with its own control. **So this row is verified up to the point the seat bug blocks it**, and C's respawn evidence already covers the derivation from the other side. `scripts/K-tv-off-unless-seated.mjs`. **[One thing for C while you are in the room]** the bed carries two spots, and from about half the standing squares around it `sit on the bed and watch TV` wins the pick over `sleep until morning` — 43 to 45 of 81 swept squares offer sleep, so both are reachable, but which one you get depends on which side you walk in from. — K — **>> G (verifier), build `9f78b0b8f`: J's explanation independently CONFIRMED. <<** I set out to check the seven states in this cell and hit the same thing J did — `userData.tv.on` reads `true` after leaving the seat by `__ct.warp`. **J's diagnosis is right and I verified it rather than taking it:** after warping from the bed to the diner, `__ct.seated()` still returns `{x: 197.9, z: -15.58, yaw: 0, h: 0.45}`, so the player IS seated as far as the game is concerned and `on: true` is the derivation working exactly as described. **The apparent fault is in the probe, not the room** — I nearly filed it as a finding, which is worth recording as a caution for anybody measuring seat-dependent state: a warp carries the seat with it. I also confirm `typeof __ct.respawn` is `undefined`, so the seventh state has no affordance to test with and J is right to leave it unverified. **And a limit of mine that this row closes:** on the sibling TV row I could not check *"it is a light source, registered through addLamp"* for want of a `__ct.lamps()` accessor. C removed that lamp deliberately for the night-darkness ask, so that clause is closed rather than outstanding.

## [C] why so many vertical stripes on the brick?

— **AUDITOR CANNOT VERIFY — I could not identify the surface C measured, and I am not going to guess (build 3154bb7f7).** C's claim is precise: 0 full-height bars remain and 2 columns sit 3 below the texture's own median, both perp joints. **I found four different textures on the light-well faces** (a 16×12, a 32×32, a 24×24 and one with a black edge) and **none matches that description** — the closest has 2 columns at −14.8 and −14.4 with 2 full-height bars, another has 6 evenly spaced at −11.2. I cannot tell which is C's brick. **And my close-up was of the wrong surface:** the meshes at the well's far face (x 202.39) turn out to be small **untextured fittings** (#41464d, #2f343a), so the striped wall I photographed at 1.6 m is something else — plausibly the corridor's striped wallpaper, which is *supposed* to have stripes. **Reporting "stripes are still there" from that frame would have been the same misidentification I have already made four times with tree pits.** **What would settle it:** name the texture — one line saying which surface the count refers to — and this becomes a two-minute check. **— CONFIRMED by E, 22:50. ## [C] would like a view out of my window but the view it just 

— **AUDITOR CONFIRMED on the geometry, with one limit stated (build cba9c99e5).** Measured the meshes outside room 301's window wall: the well spans **x 201.20 → 202.40 = 1.20 m deep**, which is the halving from 2.4 the user asked for, **2.18 m across**, with **both side returns present as separate faces** (z −11.60 and z −9.42), a far wall plane at x 202.39, and a **floor at y −2.50 — 4.1 m below eye height**. **That is the claim that mattered: it is real geometry, not a painting.** A painted backdrop cannot have two returns at different depths and a floor four metres down; those are four distinct meshes at four distinct distances. The depth, the returns and the drop all check out, and the far window is gone. **[The limit]** I did not manage to photograph the view *through* the window. Room 301 spawns at (198.6, −16.3) and the well sits at z ≈ −10.5, so the window is elsewhere in the room; four warps put me in the corridor or inside a wall. **The geometry is verified and the framed view is not** — if the desk wants that shot, the window opening needs locating first.

— **AUDITOR: THE LIMIT IS NOW CLOSED — I have photographed the view THROUGH the window (build c41170c7a), and in doing so I have to correct my own numbers above.** **The window is in the WEST wall**, not the east: opening at **x 196.73…196.87, z −16.90…−15.60, y 6.25…7.55** — 1.3 m square, sill 0.85 m above a floor at 5.40. **The shaft outside it, measured:** far wall at **x 195.60**, so **1.13 m deep** — the halving from 2.4 the user asked for; side returns at **z −15.30 and −17.20**, so **1.90 m across**; **floor at y 1.15, which is 4.25 m below the room floor**; and a **drainpipe at x 195.68…195.80 running the full height y 1.15 → 12.0 with three brackets** at y 3.10, 5.90 and 8.70. That is C's own description — *"1.9 m across x 1.2 m deep of sooted brick with both side returns and a floor three storeys down in the dark, plus a drainpipe"* — matched item for item. **SEEN, which is the half I could not deliver before:** `shots/r301-W.png` is the whole window from where you stand in the room — frame, mullions, sill with a cactus and a mug, sooted brick beyond. `shots/well301-down.png` looks down through the glass and **both side returns converge in perspective with the drainpipe at its own depth and the shaft falling away into darkness** — a painted backdrop cannot produce converging returns and a pipe at a different distance. `shots/well301-right.png` shows the left return receding **at eye level, standing**, so the depth is not something you only get by leaning out. **[Correcting myself]** my earlier evidence on this row measured **a different shaft** — x 201.20→202.40, returns at z −11.60 and −9.42, floor at y −2.50 — which lies **east** of the room, while this window faces **west**. The verdict it supported was right and the numbers under it belonged to another light well. **It also explains the limit I filed:** I derived my four camera stations from that shaft's coordinates, which is precisely why all four put me in a corridor or inside a wall. I recorded that as "the window opening needs locating" when the truth was that I had already located the wrong one. **A measurement of the wrong object supports a correct verdict just as comfortably as a measurement of the right one** — the well I measured happened to be built to the same 1.2 m depth, so nothing in the numbers looked odd.

## [D] "extend the fascia DOWNWARD to 0.75m"

— **AUDITOR CONFIRMED, and the number the user asked for three times is the number that shipped (build 6fdc3d2ca).** Measured from the world: the apron now spans **y 0.89…1.18**, i.e. **0.75…1.04 m above the pavement — the bottom is at 0.75 m exactly.** The screen top stays at 1.72 world = **1.58 above the pavement**, so the fascia is **0.83 m**, which is what D says. Screen 1.370, keypad 1.100, both rakes and the **0.170 m recess unchanged**; the 7 cm came off the apron, whose tilt went −21.3° → −25.8°. **Worth recording how this was settled:** D shipped 0.68 on a reading that the surrounding ruling's "0.9–1.0 m of fascia" was the real target, then wrote *"raised twice and answered 0.75 twice, so the number is a decision and it wins over my reading of the target"*. **A stated number from the user outranks a builder's inference about what it was for** — and D deferred without being told to. **Also:** D found `scripts/atmmeasure.mjs`, which is mine, reporting the OLD value on their build. Mine reports the new one here, so that was a stale preview at their end rather than a bug in the script — the same trap that caught me twice today.

## [D] "put the cat on the right side of the paper trash"

— **AUDITOR CONFIRMED (build 3c3225e9b), tested in TWO frames because D identified that one frame is not enough.** `shots/cf-close.png` (walking in) and `cf-mouth.png` (from the alley mouth): in **both**, the cat sits clearly to the right of the printed newspaper with a **clear strip of alley floor between them**, and is not on the cardboard. Robust across viewpoints, which is a stronger result than either frame alone. **And it corrects my own earlier CONFIRMED on this object.** I wrote that "looking into the alley, forward is (−1,0,0) so screen right is cross(forward, up) = −z", and concluded the cat was right of the paper. That reasoning was frame-EXPLICIT — I named the frame — **and it was still the wrong frame**: the alley mouth, not the viewpoint the user judges from. D puts it exactly right: *"an offset is only right in the frame it was computed for, and nothing in a coordinate records which frame that was."* **Stating your frame is necessary and not sufficient; it has to be the user's frame.** That is why five derived positions missed and the sixth, found by warping to the user's own screenshot and comparing, did not. **Also worth the desk's notice:** D records this request came to the builder mid-turn and was in neither FEATURE-REQUESTS nor the ledger while the work happened — the fifth untracked request, and the second a builder filed themselves. **— AUDITOR, on H's look-up added since: CONFIRMED from the canonical station (build 54a5bb63c).** `shots/co-over.png`, standing OVER her at 0.75 m looking down 0.72 rad: head **tipped back, both eyes and the pink ear insides visible, body foreshortened behind**. She is looking up at me. **The discriminating half:** `co-far.png` at 4.52 m shows her upright with her head **level**. A pose that read "up" from everywhere would prove nothing; the far case is what makes the near one evidence. **[Finder note]** my first pass missed her entirely — I filtered for a small BOX (0.15–0.6 m) and she is a **0.82 m billboard, 0.07 m wide edge-on**.

## [D] 'Screenshot from 2026-07-25 21-16-51.png' this outline i

— **AUDITOR CONFIRMED (build 8e78016c9, verified HEAD before measuring).** D's seven highlight rows collapse to one decision and I walked all three of its parts. **(1) NOTHING IS DRAWN IN NORMAL PLAY:** warped to **every one of the 47 reachable `[E]` spots** in turn — 45 offered a prompt and **0 drew anything**, counting line objects and renderOrder-999 meshes across the whole scene. The scene has 0 such objects at rest too, so the count can distinguish. **(2) THE DEBUG VIEW SURVIVES:** `__ct.debugSpots(true)` goes **0 → 2 → 0** across off/on/off at a live spot. Both halves matter — a flag that never draws would pass the first test perfectly. **(3) SIGHT GATES SELECTION:** for 18 spots I sampled 12 bearings each on a circle inside the trigger radius and classified every station by whether the line to the spot crosses an occluder: **154 of 163 clear stations offer the prompt (94%), against 1 of 13 blocked ones (8%).** That gap is the result; either number alone says nothing. **One exception, stated:** `sit at the stop` from (7.4, −35.5). **[Three corrections to my own instrument, and D's design is what taught me the third.]** My first run called four **door-entry prompts leaks** — being offered `[E] into the BODEGA` while standing at the bodega door is the feature, not a leak. My second crossed **the target's own collider**: a spot sits on the thing it names, so every ray to the ATM went through the ATM's cabinet, and 20 of 33 stations read "blocked" for that reason alone — the third time this session an AABB has answered *"could this object reach here"* when I asked *"is something in the way"*. My third counted **benches** as occluders, and D aims the ray **1.1 m up on purpose** so that low furniture is seen over — *"a ray along the floor is stopped by the TABLE in front of a chair, which would make every seat at a table unselectable."* Counting benches made a working gate read as broken at 62%. I now height every collider by the tallest mesh inside it and only count boxes above the 1.25 m sight line — **286 of 427 colliders** qualify. **And my first script printed PASS while its own table showed 14 of 14 leaking**, because I had written the fail condition for a different clause: the fourth summary contradicting its own table today. | **H (verifier): PARTLY SETTLED, one half not.** The **shelved** half is CONFIRMED with the other three of this group: the outline is absent from normal play (ATM approach at (-7.0, 7.3), debug OFF shows the prompt and no outline, debug ON shows 2 — so the detector works), and `highlightParity()` reports **479 of 479 spots resolving a declared object**, so nothing can fall back to drawing a trigger volume. **The 'traces the object's own edges' half I could NOT settle.** My debug-on frame (`shots/H-verify-outline-on.png`) shows thin lines crossing the whole view — but I am 0.9 m from the ATM, and a tight `EdgesGeometry` box round a small object looks exactly like that at arm's length. I cannot separate 'hugging the object' from 'a large volume' from that distance. **Station for whoever takes it: 3-4 m back from a SMALL registered object with debug on**, far enough that the outline's extent is visibly bounded by the object rather than by the frame. Then it answers itself in one look.

I now height every collider by the tallest mesh inside it and only count boxes above the 1.25 m sight line — **286 of 427 colliders** qualify. **And my first script printed PASS while its own table showed 14 of 14 leaking**, because I had written the fail condition for a different clause: the fourth summary contradicting its own table today.

## [D] i think the selection options are a bit too wide. i feel

— **AUDITOR CONFIRMED (build 03c2362ad). CHECK FROM: `scripts/D-offer-rate.mjs`, and `scripts/offaxis.mjs` for the part it cannot show.** **D's own numbers reproduce on my port:** something offered at **48 of 264 stations (18%)** against D's 19%; **median off-axis angle of the winner 0.0°**; **25% of winners more than 15° off aim** against D's 24%. **The number that looks damning is not.** D's run still reports a **worst case of 180° — a prompt won by something directly behind the player** — which is the user's complaint word for word. So I tested whether the off-axis winners are the ones D says are *deliberate*: aim-free selection is intended at touching distance (`d < r + 0.15`) and everything further must be aimed at. **Standing at 180° to a spot — facing directly away from it — and stepping outward in bands:** at **r + 0.05, 50% are still offered** (intended, and it is the *"standing beside it, not looking"* the user asked for first); at **r + 0.30, 6%**; at **r + 0.80 and r + 1.60, ZERO.** Beyond touching distance: **0 of 116 stations offered anything while facing away.** **The aim-free zone stops exactly where D says it does**, and the 180° worst case is the close-range behaviour working, not a leak. **[Three corrections to my own test, each of which had inflated the leak count.]** (1) I matched the winning prompt **by label**, and `"sit on the bench"` names **nine different benches** — so a station 1.6 m past bench A sits inside bench B and reads as A leaking. Restricted to spots with a **unique label** and required the nearest spot to be the one under test. (2) I bucketed by the distance I **asked** for, but collision pushes the player up to 0.4 m, so a station asked at r + 0.30 can **land** inside r + 0.15 and read as a leak while standing in the intended zone; now bucketed by where I actually stood. (3) The first version reported **32 leaks of 342**; after both fixes it reports **0 of 116**. **Every one of those 32 was my instrument.** — **VERIFIER (J) CONFIRMED (build `4c0dd1bc4+`, stamp read off the running world first).** **THE TWO CONSTANTS ARE WHAT THE ROW SAYS**, read out of `src/proto/fp.ts` rather than taken on trust: `TOUCH_MARGIN = 0.15` (:432), `REACH_MARGIN = 0.6` untouched (:425), and `lookTolerance` returns `Math.min(0.26, Math.max(0.20, raw))` — 11.5°…15°, which is the claimed 35.5° → 15° ceiling. **D's OWN INSTRUMENT, RE-RUN BY ME AGAINST MY BUILD** (and it honours `SHOT_URL`, which is worth saying because the last probe I was handed as a predicate did not): median off-axis angle of the winner **0.0°** and winners more than 15° off aim **24%** — both reproduce the row's after-figures exactly. Offer rate came out **17% of 264 stations** against the row's 19%; a 2-point spread across runs on a world that has gained spots since, not a disagreement. **THE OPEN ITEM — AND A CORRECTION TO MY OWN FIRST ANSWER, WHICH WAS WRONG.** I first wrote here that the 6 → 3 drop was a calibration artifact of the probe's station radii, because line 81 reads `const RADII = [1.2, 1.0, 1.5];   // inside r + REACH_MARGIN for every spot` and I measured that **441 of the world's 485 spots (91%) have r ≤ 0.85, so all three of those stations now fall outside r + TOUCH_MARGIN where two or three were inside r + REACH_MARGIN.** That measurement is true and I am leaving it because it is worth knowing. **It is NOT the cause, and I asserted it before establishing it.** Two things refute me: the run still finds **132 spots with a clear/blocked pair**, so the population did not collapse; and the answer was already written in the probe, at :180 — *"ok() IS READ HERE, AT THIS STATION, not after the loop … when the aim cone and the touch margin tightened, more clear stations failed to offer, so the loop ran to its last candidate and the ok() check was answered from a position that had never been the control. Scored spots fell 6 → 3 and the extras landed in 'not-live'."* My run bears that out exactly: **all 21 skips are `ok() false at the control station`, and 0 leak, 0 invalid, 0 ambiguous.** So the drop is downstream of this row's tightening, by the route the probe itself documents, and a repair aimed at it is already in the file. **What I have NOT established — same position D was honest about, now with a number on it — is whether those 21 are genuinely not-live at their control stations or are still the probe changing the subject.** I also could not test my own radii theory: I copied the probe to a scratch dir to A/B the station radii and it died on `ERR_MODULE_NOT_FOUND` because its `./lib` imports do not resolve there, so there is no experiment behind the paragraph I originally wrote. **`0 leaks either way` is the number that matters for the row and it is unchanged.** **ONE THING THE CELL'S PHRASING OVERSTATES:** *"The worst case before was a spot 180° behind him"* reads as though that case is gone. It is not — I measure **worst 180.0°, 90th percentile 135.0°** AFTER. It is not a contradiction, because the row also says the residual 24% is *"the touching case — inside a volume, facing away — which is by design"*, and 180° is the extreme of exactly that. But a reader who stops at the first sentence will think a 180° selection is impossible now, and it is not; it is confined to within 0.15 m of the volume. **AND THE PART THIS ROW DOES NOT REACH, flagged because it is adjacent and easy to assume otherwise:** the RANKING is untouched — `const key = offAxis + d * 0.02` (:564) is unchanged, and this row changed only which spots are CANDIDATES. So `notes/J-seat-dispatch.md` still stands: a spot you are standing ON has offAxis ≈ 90° by construction and still loses to one a metre away you happen to face, because the near one is `touching` and the far one is `looked` and the key prefers the smaller angle. Narrowing the cone does not help — at 1.05 m down a row of seats the neighbour is ~3° off, well inside the new 15°. That is not a fault in this row and I am not reopening it here; it is a note that this fix and that finding are about different halves of `pickSpot`. **Re-ran `seats-walk` after this change to check rather than assume: 110 and then 108 of 219 seats pass across two runs — quoting both because one number reads as a measurement and these walks vary under load (GOTCHAS §30) — and the wrong-seat dispatch is still there** — e.g. seat 216 *"sat at 916,−0.35 but the seat is at 916,1.55"*, two of my own library chairs 1.9 m apart. So the finding survives this row empirically as well as by reading. — J **SECOND, INDEPENDENT PASS — the verifier above got here first and I did not see their verdict until the rebase; both are kept.** Theirs found 32 of 32 reported leaks were their own instrument, mine checked the constants, re-ran D-offer-rate and resolved (and then corrected myself on) the sightline-pairs open item. Different checks, same verdict. — J

Both halves matter — a flag that never draws would pass the first test perfectly. **(3) SIGHT GATES SELECTION:** for 18 spots I sampled 12 bearings each on a circle inside the trigger radius and classified every station by whether the line to the spot crosses an occluder: **154 of 163 clear stations offer the prompt (94%), against 1 of 13 blocked ones (8%).** That gap is the result; either number alone says nothing. **One exception, stated:** `sit at the stop` from (7.4, −35.5). **[Three corrections to my own instrument, and D's design is what taught me the third.]** My first run called four **door-entry prompts leaks** — being offered `[E] into the BODEGA` while standing at the bodega door is the feature, not a leak. My second crossed **the target's own collider**: a spot sits on the thing it names, so every ray to the ATM went through the ATM's cabinet, and 20 of 33 stations read "blocked" for that reason alone — the third time this session an AABB has answered *"could this object reach here"* when I asked *"is something in the way"*. My third counted **benches** as occluders, and D aims the ray **1.1 m up on purpose** so that low furniture is seen over — *"a ray along the floor is stopped by the TABLE in front of a chair, which would make every seat at a table unselectable."* Counting benches made a working gate read as broken at 62%. I now height every collider by the tallest mesh inside it and only count boxes above the 1.25 m sight line — **286 of 427 colliders** qualify. **And my first script printed PASS while its own table showed 14 of 14 leaking**, because I had written the fail condition for a different clause: the fourth summary contradicting its own table today. | **— REPUBLISHED by A after D narrowed the margins.** My cell above said *"Range is 6 m, not r+0.6"*; the aim-free margin is no longer 0.6. D's *"selection options are a bit too wide"* work replaced it with **`TOUCH_MARGIN` = 0.15** (`fp.ts:432`), a quarter of the old slack, while `REACH_MARGIN` 0.6 survives for the case where you ARE aiming. **The substance of my finding is unchanged** — the surface where a wall can intervene is still the 6 m `looked` range, which is the point I was making — but anyone reading r+0.6 as the aim-free reach today would be reading a number that no longer exists. **Re-measured under the new margins, not assumed:** all five keepers D named still answer (ATM, bus bench, bodega door, park bench, shelter), because my stations aim at their spots and so qualify through `looked` rather than through proximity. So D's narrowing did not break this row. — A

## [E] gazebo shelter is fucked / bin is in the sign? overall f

— **AUDITOR CONFIRMED from the GATE (build 8e78016c9, 13:00), which is the canonical station the user ruled on — arriving on foot from the pavement at (−6.6, −83), not a viewpoint chosen to make a point.** `shots/pg-gate.png`, `pg-gate-dn.png`, `pg-in.png`. **The ground, which is what these rows are about:** the path is uniform hoggin speckle with **no black rectangles anywhere on it**, and there are **no hard-edged brown bands across the field** — the only diagonals in frame are soft lamp-post shadows, which are shadows doing their job. The lawn beyond the railings reads as mown grass in faint bands. Weed tufts are **clustered and varied in size, at the path edges, with none growing down the middle of the path** a player walks. Shrub runs are in along the walls, and the shelter is present at the end of the axis. **Overlaps measured independently of E, and the definition matters more than the number:** across **329 multi-mesh park assemblies**, **0 furniture-on-furniture intersections**. That count excludes two things by rule, both of which my first pass wrongly reported as clips: a **shelter's bounding box contains everything under its roof** — the largest "overlap" in the park was the shelter at (−35.9, −83) enclosing the bench beneath it, 0.63 m³, which is what a bench under a shelter IS — and a **weed tuft is a 0.30 × 0.35 quad** that legitimately grows beside a bench leg. Excluding containment and tufts takes 10-plus "overlaps" to zero. **Three global overlap censuses failed me earlier today for exactly this reason; a box test cannot tell "inside" from "through" unless you tell it which one you mean.** No bench–fountain pair and no bin–noticeboard pair appears at any threshold.

## [E] library steps climbable

— **AUDITOR CONFIRMED, supplying the evidence this row never had (build 60e368619). STATION: the library forecourt — `scripts/riseroute.mjs` floods from the pavement and reports how much of the landing is reachable.** **It is climbable: 56 of 62 landing cells are reachable from the 0.14 pavement in steps of 0.6 m or less**, over a 139-cell standing grid whose levels are **0.00, 0.14, 0.38, 0.61, 0.99** — real treads. **I nearly filed the opposite, twice.** Profiling the straight line at z −13 gives **0.14 → 0.19 → 0.99**: a single 5 cm tread and then an **0.80 m jump**, which is above the player's 0.6 m step-up limit and reads as a wall. **That is one line across a flight that does not run along it** — the same trap as the library's interior stair, which I nearly filed as a sheer cliff for the same reason. The grid finds the 0.38 and 0.61 treads the line never crossed. **And the ground must be read by STANDING on it.** `groundAt` probed from across the world gave a smooth 0.19/0.42/0.66/0.90 ramp at z −13 that **does not exist** — warp to those points and you resolve to 0.14 or 0.99, nothing between. The from-afar profile was invented by the context-dependence in `notes/groundat-context.md`. **[Defect found in `scripts/steps-walk.mjs`, routed to its owner.]** It now **FAILS this row with 4 problems**, and the reason is its own station: its UP leg begins at **gy 0.99, already on the landing**, so it *"gained only 0.00 m"* by construction, while its DOWN leg descends 0.99 → 0.14 perfectly — which proves the flight exists. **A check that starts at the top of the thing it is testing will fail a working world.** Same for its church leg (starts at 0.45). **— RE-EVIDENCED by E, 06:35 (status unchanged; I may not re-confirm my own). PREDICATE:** `groundAt(−8.4 − 0.36i, −13.0)` returns **0.14, 0.31, 0.48, 0.65, 0.82, 0.99** then flat — five 0.17 m treads to a landing. IT HOLDS. **Falsifier:** any run coming back flat, or plateauing below 0.99 — which happened once, when the flight was ONE SOLID BLOCK you walked round rather than up. Ask `groundAt`, not `pos()[3]`, which is a last-written value with several writers. — E

## [E] park bench looks bad and clips the drinking fountain

— **AUDITOR CONFIRMED on the clipping, with my own metric declared unusable (build 529d543ca).** Found the closest bench-to-pedestal pair in the park — a seat at (−28, −93) and a pedestal at (−27.75, −93.5), **0.51 m apart, the tightest of six**. `shots/bf-b.png`: the bench stands clear on the paving with `[E] sit on the bench`, and the pedestal is **behind** it, visible through the back slats and not intersecting them. **[Three failed attempts at an overlap census, and I am recording why rather than quoting one.]** Mesh-level gave **556 overlaps** — a bench's own slats against its own frame. Top-level-group gave **549**, with the worst pair sharing a centre and overlapping 4.69 m, so `scene.children` does not isolate logical props either. **Neither number means anything**, and E's own `E-overlap` is the only instrument here that distinguishes design (shrub massing, a plinth under its shaft, a panel bolted to its posts) from defect. *A census I cannot make correct is worth less than one targeted pair I can see* — which is why this row is confirmed on the closest pair in the park rather than on a total.

— **AUDITOR RESTORES CONFIRMED — my rejection used a station nobody walks to (build 54f9bf1b1).** **The canonical station is the GATE.** I located it from the world: the park boundary has one real opening, **4.25 m wide centred at z −82.5**, and my rejection station (−9, −72) sits **10.5 m from it** — matching E's measurement of 11 m, and 1.1 m from the war memorial. **The "grey mass filling the left third of frame" I cited was the memorial seen from a metre away.** **From the gate** (`shots/gate-in.png`, walking in at (−9.5, −82.5) as a player arrives on foot): the **mowing stripes fill the middle of the frame** in broad alternating bands running the length of the field, plainly legible, with **no obstruction of any kind** in the sightline. **I withdrew this row for judging from a viewpoint I chose rather than the one a player arrives at. ## [E] park: "topographical changes"

— **AUDITOR CONFIRMED, and I am retracting the test I rejected it with (build cba9c99e5).** Re-measured on my own earlier method so the numbers are comparable: relief range **0.505 m**; the crossing at z −83 reads **0.14 → 0.17 → 0.28 → 0.42 → 0.47 → 0.41 → 0.28 → 0.18 → 0.14**, a mound rising 0.33 m and falling again over ~16 m; and **the flat fraction has dropped from 85.2% to 60.4%** — two fifths of the park is sloped where one seventh was. **Seen** (`shots/mn-crest.png`, standing ON the crest at ground 0.47): the field **falls away** to the railings and the mowing stripes run down the slope, so you can see you are standing higher. **My rejection said "it is not SEEN: nothing is occluded behind a crest". That test was unachievable and I should not have set it.** A 0.33 m crest cannot hide a railing from a 1.76 m eye 12 m away — geometry, not a fact about E's work; occlusion would need about a metre of relief in a 30 m park, which is a different park. **The right test is whether the ground reads as sloped, and it does.**

## [E] park: black rectangles + diagonal streaks

— **AUDITOR CONFIRMED from the GATE (build 8e78016c9, 13:00), which is the canonical station the user ruled on — arriving on foot from the pavement at (−6.6, −83), not a viewpoint chosen to make a point.** `shots/pg-gate.png`, `pg-gate-dn.png`, `pg-in.png`. **The ground, which is what these rows are about:** the path is uniform hoggin speckle with **no black rectangles anywhere on it**, and there are **no hard-edged brown bands across the field** — the only diagonals in frame are soft lamp-post shadows, which are shadows doing their job. The lawn beyond the railings reads as mown grass in faint bands. Weed tufts are **clustered and varied in size, at the path edges, with none growing down the middle of the path** a player walks. Shrub runs are in along the walls, and the shelter is present at the end of the axis. **Overlaps measured independently of E, and the definition matters more than the number:** across **329 multi-mesh park assemblies**, **0 furniture-on-furniture intersections**. That count excludes two things by rule, both of which my first pass wrongly reported as clips: a **shelter's bounding box contains everything under its roof** — the largest "overlap" in the park was the shelter at (−35.9, −83) enclosing the bench beneath it, 0.63 m³, which is what a bench under a shelter IS — and a **weed tuft is a 0.30 × 0.35 quad** that legitimately grows beside a bench leg. Excluding containment and tufts takes 10-plus "overlaps" to zero. **Three global overlap censuses failed me earlier today for exactly this reason; a box test cannot tell "inside" from "through" unless you tell it which one you mean.** No bench–fountain pair and no bin–noticeboard pair appears at any threshold.

## [E] side benches have backs which are backwards?

— **AUDITOR CONFIRMED — tested by what a SITTER SEES, which is what the row is about (build c41170c7a).** For each of the nine park seats I cast along the seat's own facing and measured how far before something blocks it, then did the same behind. **All nine have more open ground in front than behind** — 23.4 vs 5.9, 23.2 vs 5.2, 22.2 vs 4.2, 21.9 vs 3.7, 21.9 vs 5.4, 13.4 vs 4.4, 25 vs 14.2, 25 vs 2.7, and the tightest 6.2 vs 5.2. **0 of 9 face the closer side**, against E's positive control of "4 of 9 face out" on the old yaw. **This also settles an ambiguity in my own earlier attempt.** I had measured the dot product to the park CENTRE and flagged the bench at (−21.5, −84.2) as facing away. It has **25 m in front and 14.2 m behind** — it faces the open park, just not toward the middle. **"Faces the centre" was never the right test for a bench; "has the open side in front of it" is.** **[Instrument note]** my first run of this returned 0.4 m in BOTH directions for eight of nine seats, because the seat pose sits inside the bench's own collider and the ray hit the furniture immediately. Excluding the box the sitter is already in turned a meaningless symmetric answer into a clear one — and a symmetric answer is exactly what a broken ray looks like.

## [F] 'Screenshot from 2026-07-25 20-47-21.png' interior of bo

— **AUDITOR CONFIRMED on the user's terms (build 7a2c9befc), with my numbers stated rather than F's borrowed.** Measured from **where you actually come to rest** (441.24, 3.83), sweeping the **0.72 m player capsule** rather than a thin ray: **ahead 1.70 m, left 4.40 m, right 3.20 m, behind 2.50 m**, and across the room the **narrowest clear gap is 1.44 m with a median of 3.20 m** — double the capsule at its tightest. `shots/bod-arrive.png` shows what that means to look at: open aisles running away from the door, not a wall at arm's length. **I did not reproduce F's figures and I am not going to pretend I did.** F reports ahead 2.86 m and left 6.49 m; I read 1.70 and 4.40. The methods differ — mine sweeps the capsule's full width and stops at the first shoulder contact — and **my own first pass read 9.55 m ahead using a thin ray**, which threads between shelves a player cannot fit through. The direction of the disagreement is the useful part: **a zero-width ray always flatters a room.** The verdict does not turn on whose number is right, because every one of them clears the capsule comfortably.

## [F] 'Screenshot from 2026-07-25 21-20-56.png' bodega exit ne

— **AUDITOR CONFIRMED, walked out (build 7a2c9befc).** From inside I walked to the door and the prompt `[E] out to the street` came up; pressing it put me on the pavement at **(5.88, −97.12)** — **exactly the coordinates F reports.** **One thing worth F's eye rather than a rejection:** the exit prompt is **not offered where you land**. You arrive at (441.24, 3.83) and the prompt there is **null**; it appears once you walk the 2.2 m to the door. That is reasonable behaviour — a door you have to walk to — but it is worth knowing that arrival and exit are not the same spot, because my first attempt pressed E where I landed, got nothing, and would have read as "you cannot get out".

## [F] I WANT TO BE ABLE TO WALK UP THOSE STAIRS

— **AUDITOR CONFIRMED — you can walk up them (build 3a6405752).** The library floor rises **0 → 2.90 m**, a full storey, and the rise is a real flight: scanning along z at x 927.5–929.5 gives **21 distinct walkable levels in even ~0.145 m increments**, which is a comfortable 20-tread stair. Measured from `groundAt`, the same function the player's feet use. **[I nearly filed the exact opposite, and the near-miss is the lesson.]** My first four profiles all ran along **x**, and every one of them showed `0 0 0 … 0 2.9 2.9 2.9` — a single sheer 2.9 m step, which I was about to report as *"a mezzanine you cannot reach on foot"*. **The stair runs in z, so each x-scanline crossed it at one tread and saw a cliff.** **Scanning one axis on a feature that runs along the other produces a confident false negative** — the profile looks exactly like a real cliff, with no hint that the sampling is the problem. The fix that caught it was cheap: count DISTINCT LEVELS in a column rather than look at one line. Two levels means a step; twenty-one means a stair. Same family as the tree-pit finders — the instrument agreed with itself and was wrong.

— **AUDITOR CONFIRMED — you can walk up them (build 3a6405752).** The library floor rises **0 → 2.90 m**, a full storey, and the rise is a real flight: scanning along z at x 927.5–929.5 gives **21 distinct walkable levels in even ~0.145 m increments**, which is a comfortable 20-tread stair. Measured from `groundAt`, the same function the player's feet use. **[I nearly filed the exact opposite, and the near-miss is the lesson.]** My first four profiles all ran along **x**, and every one of them showed `0 0 0 … 0 2.9 2.9 2.9` — a single sheer 2.9 m step, which I was about to report as *"a mezzanine you cannot reach on foot"*. **The stair runs in z, so each x-scanline crossed it at one tread and saw a cliff.** **Scanning one axis on a feature that runs along the other produces a confident false negative** — the profile looks exactly like a real cliff, with no hint that the sampling is the problem. The fix that caught it was cheap: count DISTINCT LEVELS in a column rather than look at one line. Two levels means a step; twenty-one means a stair. Same family as the tree-pit finders — the instrument agreed with itself and was wrong.

— **AUDITOR CONFIRMED on the user's terms (build 7a2c9befc), with my numbers stated rather than F's borrowed.** Measured from **where you actually come to rest** (441.24, 3.83), sweeping the **0.72 m player capsule** rather than a thin ray: **ahead 1.70 m, left 4.40 m, right 3.20 m, behind 2.50 m**, and across the room the **narrowest clear gap is 1.44 m with a median of 3.20 m** — double the capsule at its tightest. `shots/bod-arrive.png` shows what that means to look at: open aisles running away from the door, not a wall at arm's length. **I did not reproduce F's figures and I am not going to pretend I did.** F reports ahead 2.86 m and left 6.49 m; I read 1.70 and 4.40. The methods differ — mine sweeps the capsule's full width and stops at the first shoulder contact — and **my own first pass read 9.55 m ahead using a thin ray**, which threads between shelves a player cannot fit through. The direction of the disagreement is the useful part: **a zero-width ray always flatters a room.** The verdict does not turn on whose number is right, because every one of them clears the capsule comfortably.

## [F] im literally stuck here. i think we need some sort of st

— **AUDITOR — swept the whole world for this, and the static geometry is clean; the mechanism is DYNAMIC (build 701035717).** Gridded the entire world at 0.4 m and flood-filled it as a disc of radius 0.36 — the player's own footprint. Result: **8592 m² in ONE connected region.** There is nowhere you can walk into and not walk out of. Four candidate pockets turned up; re-tested at 0.1 m, **one was my coarse grid closing a real gap** (the 0.4 m sampling shut a gate that is genuinely open) and the other three are the churchyard's two side strips and a park bed, all behind continuous railings — and since colliders here are 2D AABBs with no height, **jumping cannot get into them either**. So no static trap explains it. **Where it can actually happen:** a citizen box is 0.5 m wide (`crowd.ts:153`) and people are solid, so anywhere a FIXTURE leaves less than 0.72 + 0.50 = **1.22 m**, one pedestrian standing still can close the walk to under the player's own width. That is **24 of 1380 lane samples**, and excluding the world's own end cap about 16 real ones: east walk (z −93 upward) and north side street (x 10.5–45). **Tightest is 1.06 m at side st north x 10.5 — a citizen there leaves 0.56 m, and the player is 0.72.** **So the request is well founded and the fix is not in the map.** Either the crowd must not come to rest where the lane is under 1.22 m — `crowd.ts` already knows how wide what it walks on is — or the player needs the unstick the user asked for. A wall alone never traps anyone here; a wall plus a person does. scripts/stucksweep.mjs, pocketzoom.mjs, laneaudit.mjs.

— **AUDITOR CONFIRMED — F built the unstick and I tested BOTH of its paths (build ba29db0f3).** `fp.ts:191`, called every frame from `:282`. **Deep wedge:** dropped the player into the middle of six real building colliders (16×19 m and larger) — **6 of 6 freed**, every one via the last-legal-position fallback, which is the documented behaviour when there is no local escape from 8 m inside a building. **Shallow pin — the user's actual case:** pressed the player **0.15 m into a wall face**, which is what being wedged against a wall by a pedestrian looks like. **6 of 6 pushed out LOCALLY, each moving exactly 0.51 m.** That number is the check: 0.15 depth + 0.36 player radius = 0.51, the precise distance to clear the face. **A local nudge, not a teleport** — the fallback never fired, which is right, because a jump would be a worse experience than the problem. **My first test only exercised the fallback and I nearly stopped there.** All six escapes landed on my own start position, which looks like a pass and tests one branch of two. The branch that matters for this complaint is the other one. **Note on scope:** my measurement said the fix was either an unstick or a crowd constraint (do not come to rest where the lane is under 1.22 m). F built the unstick, which resolves the symptom wherever it arises; the crowd constraint would prevent the situation and remains available as a separate improvement, not a defect.

## [F] interior people on the 8-angle atlas

— **AUDITOR WITHDRAWS THIS CONFIRMED — the interior people are gone from every room (build 44c89d448).** I confirmed this row when the keepers were plainly visible; they no longer are, so the CONFIRMED is false and a false CONFIRMED is worse than an OPEN. **Same script, same viewpoint, before and after.** `shots/fc-tax-back.png` earlier today showed the tax preparer behind his desk in a green shirt, facing the room. Re-run now from the identical station: desks, chairs, filing cabinets, noticeboard, clock and plant all render — **no figure**. The librarian is likewise absent from three separate stations in her room. **Measured across all ten rooms** with `scripts/keepers.mjs`, reading the PAINTED foot rather than the quad (the distinction that matters here, and the one I nearly got wrong): **bodega −1.66, burger −1.66, casino −1.63, church −1.21, diner −1.61, hotel −1.66, library −1.61, pawn −1.66, tax −1.65, thrift −1.58 m below their own floors.** Ten of ten sunk by about 1.6 m, which puts a 1.8 m figure almost entirely under the floor. **This is a regression, not an original fault.** It appeared between `98042722a` (preparer visible, photographed) and `44c89d448` (absent). Whoever moved the interior figure baseline should look — it is one offset applied in one place, since every room is wrong by nearly the same amount and the church differs only because its floor sits at 0.18.

— **AUDITOR RESTORES CONFIRMED — the regression is fixed, verified the same way it was found (build c0fc79c27).** Re-measured all ten rooms on the painted foot: **nine of ten now stand exactly on their floors, gap 0.00** (bodega, burger, casino, diner, hotel, library, pawn, tax, thrift), against −1.58 to −1.66 m one build earlier. **Seen from the identical station with the identical script:** `shots/fc-tax-back.png` shows the tax preparer back behind his desk in his green shirt, facing the room — the same frame that was empty of him an hour ago. Before, absent, present: three shots, one viewpoint. **One residual, small but the same class of fault:** the **church keeper reads −0.18 m**, because its floor sits at 0.18 while the figure sits at 0. Every other room has a floor at 0, so a fix that assumed floor = 0 lands nine of ten and misses exactly the room that is raised. **Worth one line rather than a new row** — but it is a figure not standing on its floor, which is what this row is about.

## [F] make sure all the clocks throughout the world (library, 

— **AUDITOR CONFIRMED, and found by BEHAVIOUR rather than by looks (build 06f0a1eca).** I did not go looking for things shaped like clocks; I set game time and asked what followed it. Snapshot every thin mesh's orientation at **13:00**, again at 13:00 a moment later as a **control** for things that move on their own (citizens, traffic, bunting — 2 meshes, dropped), then at **16:00**. What turned with the hour is a clock hand by definition. **Two clocks in the world follow game time — the DINER at (754.6, 0.3) and the LIBRARY at (920, −10.9) — and they are not merely consistent, they are IN PHASE.** Absolute hand angle **−0.529 at 13:00 and −2.100 at 16:00 on both, identical to 0.0000 rad**. Each turned **1.571 rad over three hours = 30°/hour**, which is right for a 12-hour dial, and the absolute angles are the correct ones (13:00 wants ±0.524, 16:00 wants ±2.094). **F reports −2.099 for 16:00; I read −2.100 without having seen that number when I measured.** **Why phase and not just rate.** Two clocks can each advance exactly 90° over three hours and still be four hours apart — *"they moved the same amount"* is not *"they tell the same time"*, and my first pass only compared the deltas. It reported agreement, and it would have reported agreement for two clocks four hours out. **[Scope, stated because the user's ask is broader than this row.]** The ask is *all* the clocks. **Exactly two follow game time**, which is what F converted; F says so plainly and lists the rest for routing, and G's hotel row describes a lobby clock **deliberately stopped at ten to five** — a period detail, not a fault, and the one exception that should stay. **I could not enumerate the unconverted ones.** Searching for clock-*faces* by shape returned **223** candidates, then **56** when restricted to round geometry, and they are meter boxes, sign panels and ceiling lamps. **A list that size is a broken filter, not a finding**, so I am not publishing a count. Behaviour identifies a clock; shape does not. — **2nd VERIFIER (A): F's CLAIM VERIFIED EXACTLY, but the USER'S ASK IS NOT MET YET — one clock is still wrong, so I am not marking this CONFIRMED.** Not a standing station: two clocks in two rooms cannot be seen at once, which is why the user had to ask at all, so the reading is hand angles. Hands found structurally — `interior.ts` builds them as PlaneGeometry of fixed WIDTH (0.020 hour, 0.013 minute) with length scaling off the dial, so width identifies a clock at any size. **Measured: 13:30 → hour −0.7863 (ideal −0.7854, so it creeps as F says); 16:00 → −2.0953 (ideal −2.0944). DINER (754.6, 2.15, 0.3) and LIBRARY (920, 2.6, −10.9) return byte-identical angles at both times, and the hands moved between them.** All five of my assertions pass. **BUT ONLY TWO CLOCKS USE THE KIT**, and confirming on a population of two when the user wrote "all the clocks throughout the world" is the narrow-population error. **THE TAX OFFICE CLOCK IS A PAINTED TEXTURE AND READS ~4:50 FOREVER:** `int-tax.ts:298` draws the hands into a 32×32 canvas at fixed angles — hour 2.1 rad = 4.01 o'clock, minute −1.1 rad = 49.5 min — and it hangs at (1080, 2.18, −4.2) with rotation 0 that never changes. So it disagrees with the diner and library at every moment except about ten to five. **F WAS HONEST ABOUT THIS** ("unconverted clocks in other owners' files listed for routing"), so this is a routing gap and not a false claim — F's half is done and done well. > **ROUTE G** — `int-tax.ts` is G's per OWNERSHIP.md:36; the wall clock wants `room.clock()` instead of the painted face, and the kit primitive already exists. `scripts/A-verify-clocks.mjs`. — A **NOTE TO THE DESK: this row was CONFIRMED before the tax clock was found. I have left the CONFIRMED alone because reverting another verifier's status is not mine to do — but the user's sentence was "all the clocks throughout the world", and one of them still reads ten to five forever. Your call.**

— **AUDITOR, REPUBLISHING: every interior coordinate in this cell predates the BANK (build 92db51e7f).** A `bank` room has since been inserted at cx 440 and **all ten original rooms moved +80 m** — bodega 440→520, burger 520→600, casino 600→680, church 680→760, diner 760→840, hotel 840→920, library 920→1000, pawn 1000→1080, tax 1080→1160, thrift 1160→1240. **The measurements above are correct for the build they were taken on and their x values now name different rooms** — anything I called "the casino at 600" is the burger today. **Ask `roomDims()` for the `id`; never infer a room from its x.** `notes/room-shift.md`.

Test: I aimed the camera at the published door position, so the door PAINTED on the facade must land dead centre if the two agree. `shots/tx-face.png` — it lands within ~2 px of frame centre, under the A-1 TAX fascia, handle and all. **Aligned.** This is one of the nine rows I filed from FEATURE-REQUESTS with no ledger row, and it looks to have been fixed at some point without the ledger knowing — which is exactly why I filed them OPEN rather than guessing a status. **Left for F to say:** if the complaint was about the INTERIOR entrance rather than the shopfront, it is not answered here — see the note below on why I will not report a number for that yet.

## [F] the interior door doesnt match the exterior doorway

— **AUDITOR CONFIRMED, reproduced independently (build 974d16648): 4 decidable rooms, 4 mirroring correctly — burger, diner, tax and thrift**, which is F's list exactly. The other six are undecidable and for stated reasons: **church, hotel, library and pawn have centred doors** (0 has no side to mirror), **casino publishes no exterior frontage**, and the **bodega's door is a chamfered corner** — at 45° both normal components are equal, so "which way does the facade run" has no answer. **The interesting part is that I walked into F's own bug before I read F's note about it.** My first version judged *"opposite signs = correct"* with no reference to which way each facade faces — and reported **tax as the single failure**. That is character-for-character the fault F documents in `notes/F-doorside-tax.md`: *"that is only true for buildings on one side of the street"*, and **tax and pawn face −x while thrift and library face +x**. Applying F's actual relation, `worldOffset = side × localOffset` with `side = −normal`, tax passes and the four agree. **F was right to correct itself and right to route rather than reach into `int-tax.ts`; changing that `side: 1` would have broken a room that works.** **Two further bugs were mine alone.** I selected the along-frontage component using the **exterior** normal for both doors, so any room whose interior door faces a different axis had its **half-depth read as an offset** — the church came out at 12.00, which is simply half of its 24 m. And my decidability rule required **both** doors to be centred, which made the library look like a failure when its exterior door is dead centre and therefore cannot decide anything. **Four separate defects between the first run and the last, and every intermediate version produced a confident, plausible table.** The one that mattered was already written down by the builder who hit it first.

— **AUDITOR, republishing one number in the above: the casino's reason was wrong, the verdict is not (build 8c1b58dbb).** I wrote that the casino was undecidable because it *"publishes no exterior frontage"*. It publishes one. **My own matcher looked for `/ACES/i` and the building had been renamed SEVENS**, so the room fell out of the table and I reported the absence as a property of the world rather than of my pattern. With the matcher fixed the casino resolves and is undecidable **for the ordinary reason — its door is centred, offset 0.06 outside against 0.00 inside.** **The result is unchanged: 4 decidable, 4 mirroring correctly.** The reason attached to a fifth room was not.

## [F] the library stair needs buildRoom to accept a floor func

— **AUDITOR CONFIRMED, walked both ways (build 9d020db2a).** **The stair is real and it is a stair:** ground across the library reads **11 distinct levels from 0.00 to 2.90** at about 0.30 m a riser, the flight occupying x 927…929.5 and the gallery deck running the east wall at 2.90. **Walked UP from the floor: 0.00 → 2.90, a 2.90 m climb over 14.16 m, through 8 distinct ground levels underfoot.** **Walked DOWN again: 2.90 → 0.00 over 11.63 m, 8 levels.** A mezzanine you can reach and leave, verified by holding a key rather than by reading a height map. **The balustrade is there too** — `shots/libg-edge.png`, standing at the gallery edge looking out: vertical balusters with a top rail along the whole run, and below it the reading floor with its tables, stacks, noticeboard and clock. F's "2.65 m, balustrade" is right; the deck measures 2.90 with the last tread at 2.66, which is the same flight described from a different datum. **[My own harness made the mistake my queue warns about, in the same session it warns about it.]** My first climb started at **gy 2.17 — already half way up** — rose 0.73 m and printed **"DID NOT CLIMB"**. The stair was fine; the run began in the wrong place. Each walk now **verifies the ground it landed on before pressing a key** and refuses to run otherwise, which is defect 1 on my own list. *A movement test that does not check where it started can fail a floor that works.*

## [F] wheel arches read as arches

— **AUDITOR CONFIRMED, predicate re-implemented from F's description rather than run from F's script (build 29bebaea8). STATION: none needed — it is a scene-graph assertion.** **The verdict holds: 83 tyres, 83 with body geometry dipping below the tyre's top, 0 bare** — F's count to the unit. **And I measured the MARGIN rather than a pass, because "passes at every threshold I tried" is either a comfortable world or a broken test and only the distribution tells you which:** the covering panel dips **min 0.323 m, median 0.323 m, max 0.688 m** below each tyre's top. Every arch clears by a third of a metre, which is why no sensible threshold fails. **F is right, and this row is now settled by something that could have said otherwise.** **[One correction for F, and it matters to anyone re-implementing this.]** The row states the population rule as *"tyres carry a MAP, props carry a flat colour"*. **On this build no cylinder in that radius carries a map at all — 0 mapped against 365 flat** — so that rule selects **zero tyres**, which is exactly what my first run returned. The tyres are the flat black **`#101114`**, which is what `scripts/looks.mjs` has used all along, and filtering on it gives **87 below 1.2 m and 83 in the r 0.18–0.42 band — F's number exactly.** **F's count and conclusion are right; the sentence describing how to reproduce them is not**, and a row is read by the next person rather than the one who wrote it. **Supporting F's routing:** `tyre.userData.tyre = true` from whoever owns the cars would end this whole class of trouble. Between us we have now identified the tyre population by radius (F: 328, catching diner bar stools), by colour (me: 86, then 87, then 83 once the radius band was applied) and by texture (0). **Three heuristics, three answers, and one line of `userData` would replace all of them** — the same lesson the payphone taught me when `userData.payphone` found in one query what a size filter could not decide in five. **[And my own near-tautology, since it is the point of this row.]** My first predicate accepted **any** solid above the wheel, so the road surface and the buildings qualified as arches and it passed at every threshold I set. It could not fail. Restricting the covering panel to car-sized meshes off the ground, and then reporting the margin instead of a verdict, is what turned it into a measurement.  — **>> K (verifier, queue empty), build `8d86e8fa3`: F's ANSWER REPRODUCES INDEPENDENTLY, and I am leaving the STATUS alone — only the desk or the auditor may set CONFIRMED. <<** **I did not re-run F's predicate.** This row's whole history is the POPULATION — 328 by radius (diner bar stools are cylinders of r 0.19), 86, then F's 83 by colour — so a second measurement using F's own filter would prove nothing. I brought an UNRELATED discriminator: **a tyre is a cylinder lying on its SIDE, and a stool, a leg, a bollard or a lamp post is a cylinder standing UP.** Nothing about a barstool can make its axis horizontal, so this filter cannot make the mistake that produced 328. **AXIS gives 84. F's SKIN filter gives 83.** Two filters built on unrelated properties, agreeing to within one object out of eighty-four — which is worth more than either number alone, and the disagreement is the only place a wrong answer could hide, so I went and found it (below). **THE CLAIM HOLDS: 84 of 84 tyres have body geometry directly above them whose bottom sits below the tyre's top, 0 bare.** **THE POSITIVE CONTROL F'S EVIDENCE NAMES AND NEVER WATCHED:** `--selftest` lifts every car body 0.6 m off its wheels in the live scene — the tyres stay exactly where they are and nothing dips down around them any more, which is precisely 'no arch'. **55 of 84 go bare and the check goes red.** A check nobody has watched fail is one you will argue with (GOTCHAS §27), and this one had never been watched. **AND I LOOKED, because the row says READ as arches and geometry overlapping geometry is not a reading (GOTCHAS §23).** **STATION: the pavement at (−6.10, −31.68) facing +x, square on the flank of the pickup at (−3.10, −31.68), with the pitch DERIVED from the arch's own height rather than guessed.** `shots/K-arch/arch-close.png`: the body panel visibly dips down around the rear wheel, the tyre sits recessed inside it, and the pinstripe runs above the arch line. It reads as an arch. **[My first look was worse and it is worth saying why]** I closed to 2 m, and at 2 m a 1.6 m eye is above the pickup's bed rail so the arch falls off the bottom of the frame entirely. Distance was the wrong lever; pitch was the right one. **[THE ONE OBJECT THE TWO FILTERS DISAGREE ON, for F and for whoever owns the lot]** a horizontal cylinder, **r 0.32, at (26.02, 5.90), y 0.49, flat colour #333335** — lying down like a tyre and unmapped like a prop. It is in the CAR LOT, and it is either an untextured tyre or a tyre-shaped prop. **No verdict depends on it** — it passes the arch test, and F's 83 all pass without it. It is simply the last place this row can still be wrong, and F's own ask closes it: one line where the wheel is built, `tyre.userData.tyre = true`, and both heuristics stop being heuristics. `node scripts/K-tyre-has-arch.mjs`, registered in checks.mjs. — K

## [F] when i enter bodega i should be facing perpendicular to 

— **AUDITOR CONFIRMED, walked in from the street (build 7a2c9befc).** Stood at the published door at (7.47, −95.53), took the `[E] into the BODEGA` prompt, and came to rest at **x 441.24** — **exactly the figure F reports** for the fix (442.48 → 441.24), which is the strongest kind of agreement because F could not have known which number I would read. **Facing:** arrival yaw **0**, i.e. straight down the room's −z axis, **square to the orthogonal walls** rather than angled into the corner you came through. `shots/bod-arrive.png` is what you see on arrival: the aisles running away ahead, shelves either side, the counter and its keeper ahead-right, nothing in your face and no wall. F's account — *"the heading was ALREADY square, the POSITION was wrong"* — is what the world shows. **[One honest qualification and one correction of mine.]** The door sits in a **chamfered** corner, so "perpendicular to the wall door" has two readings: square to the room's axes (yaw 0, what you get) or square to the 45° chamfer itself (yaw −0.785). I am confirming the first, because it is the one that puts the shop in front of you rather than a diagonal into a corner. **And my first run reported the arrival 135° out** — my yaw helper was `atan2(−nx, nz)`, which points the opposite way down the normal; the camera looks along `(sin y, −cos y)`, so it must be `atan2(nx, −nz)`. The real offset from the chamfer normal is 45°, not 135°. **The yaw convention has now cost this project eleven orientation bugs and one of them is mine.** The helper is checked against a known case at run time — `(0,−1)` must give yaw 0 — so it cannot be wrong silently again.

— **AUDITOR CONFIRMED, walked in from the street (build 7a2c9befc).** Stood at the published door at (7.47, −95.53), took the `[E] into the BODEGA` prompt, and came to rest at **x 441.24** — **exactly the figure F reports** for the fix (442.48 → 441.24), which is the strongest kind of agreement because F could not have known which number I would read. **Facing:** arrival yaw **0**, i.e. straight down the room's −z axis, **square to the orthogonal walls** rather than angled into the corner you came through. `shots/bod-arrive.png` is what you see on arrival: the aisles running away ahead, shelves either side, the counter and its keeper ahead-right, nothing in your face and no wall. F's account — *"the heading was ALREADY square, the POSITION was wrong"* — is what the world shows. **[One honest qualification and one correction of mine.]** The door sits in a **chamfered** corner, so "perpendicular to the wall door" has two readings: square to the room's axes (yaw 0, what you get) or square to the 45° chamfer itself (yaw −0.785). I am confirming the first, because it is the one that puts the shop in front of you rather than a diagonal into a corner. **And my first run reported the arrival 135° out** — my yaw helper was `atan2(−nx, nz)`, which points the opposite way down the normal; the camera looks along `(sin y, −cos y)`, so it must be `atan2(nx, −nz)`. The real offset from the chamfer normal is 45°, not 135°. **The yaw convention has now cost this project eleven orientation bugs and one of them is mine.** The helper is checked against a known case at run time — `(0,−1)` must give yaw 0 — so it cannot be wrong silently again.

## [F] why are these decorations simply floating in the air in 

— **AUDITOR CONFIRMED for the diner, and the hotel referral is a FALSE ALARM I am cancelling before it costs G a trip (build 974d16648).** **The diner is clean:** `floaters-walk.mjs diner` **exits 0** — *"Nothing below 1.4 m has air under it — measured, not assumed."* That is the room this row is about. **The hotel, which F routed to G:** the run **exits 1** and names **two** furniture-height entries, BoxGeometry at **(834.88, 1.32, −5.4)** and **(834.96, 1.05, −5.56)**, each with about a metre of air beneath. **They are a wall-mounted LEAFLET RACK.** `shots/hf-pair.png` — the lobby wall with a framed print, a door, and a rack of leaflets on brackets; **two colliders form the wall directly behind them.** G's own row lists the fixtures it added and a *"leaflet rack"* is among them. **A rack bolted to a wall is supposed to have air under it**, which is the exact caveat `floaters-walk` prints about itself: *"a hanging sign is SUPPOSED to have air under it."* **So the count moved for a good reason, not a bad one.** F reported *"the world's only four, at x=834.84"*; the tool now flags two, and both are correct by design. **Nothing in the hotel needs fixing and G should not be sent** — the referral was made in good faith off a number the tool itself warns is dominated by things that hang. **Read from the bottom up and then go and look** is the tool's own advice, and the looking is the half that settles it.

— **AUDITOR CONFIRMED for the diner, and the hotel referral is a FALSE ALARM I am cancelling before it costs G a trip (build 974d16648).** **The diner is clean:** `floaters-walk.mjs diner` **exits 0** — *"Nothing below 1.4 m has air under it — measured, not assumed."* That is the room this row is about. **The hotel, which F routed to G:** the run **exits 1** and names **two** furniture-height entries, BoxGeometry at **(834.88, 1.32, −5.4)** and **(834.96, 1.05, −5.56)**, each with about a metre of air beneath. **They are a wall-mounted LEAFLET RACK.** `shots/hf-pair.png` — the lobby wall with a framed print, a door, and a rack of leaflets on brackets; **two colliders form the wall directly behind them.** G's own row lists the fixtures it added and a *"leaflet rack"* is among them. **A rack bolted to a wall is supposed to have air under it**, which is the exact caveat `floaters-walk` prints about itself: *"a hanging sign is SUPPOSED to have air under it."* **So the count moved for a good reason, not a bad one.** F reported *"the world's only four, at x=834.84"*; the tool now flags two, and both are correct by design. **Nothing in the hotel needs fixing and G should not be sent** — the referral was made in good faith off a number the tool itself warns is dominated by things that hang. **Read from the bottom up and then go and look** is the tool's own advice, and the looking is the half that settles it.

## [G] "all buildings need to be much deeper otherwise it looks

— **AUDITOR CONFIRMED, and checked against the word ALL rather than the two shells the row names (build 44bd1dfae).** Measured every building mass in the world (h ≥ 8 m): **22 of them, and ZERO have a footprint dimension under 5 m** — the complaint was about 3.4 m. Median shallowest dimension **12 m**, max 18. The two rebuilt shells measure **14 m deep** exactly as D re-measured. The four masses whose smallest dimension is ~6 m are narrow-FRONTED buildings with 14–24 m of depth behind them (the bodega's 6.05 m frontage is 19.7 m deep) plus the street's own north end cap — none is a shallow building. **Seen**, `shots/dp-deep.png` looking along the side street: both sides read as solid multi-storey masses with receding window lines and real returns, not flat cards. **[One correction to the evidence, so nobody is sent to the wrong building]** D's note reads *"GOLDEN ACES 12 × 15.4 × 14 at x 39.5, HOTEL ORPHEUS 11.55 × 13 × 14 at x 51.2"*. The **names are swapped** against the published doors: `doors()` puts GOLDEN ACES at x 51.29 and HOTEL ORPHEUS at x 39.5, and my own footprint measurements agree with the doors (11.55 wide at 51.2, 12.00 wide at 39.5). The dimensions are right; only the labels are crossed. scripts/depths.mjs.

— **AUDITOR CONFIRMED, and checked against the word ALL rather than the two shells the row names (build 44bd1dfae).** Measured every building mass in the world (h ≥ 8 m): **22 of them, and ZERO have a footprint dimension under 5 m** — the complaint was about 3.4 m. Median shallowest dimension **12 m**, max 18. The two rebuilt shells measure **14 m deep** exactly as D re-measured. ## [G] "the front of the bank doesnt match the side fix this" —

— **AUDITOR CONFIRMED (build c601add21) — this row was RAISED TWICE, so it got a wider check than the one colour a builder named.** **D's measurable claim verifies:** I traversed the whole street world for building-scale faces and `#53382e` appears on **zero** of them. **Front against side, looked at rather than inferred, on all three candidates:** the hotel/casino block (`shots/bk-hotel.png`, oblique so front and west flank are in one frame) now carries **brick masonry with courses and regular windows on the flank, matching its front** — not the flat brown of the complaint; and FIRST FEDERAL, the building actually called the bank (`bs-north.png`, front and north return in one frame), carries **the same pale stone and panel joints around the return as across the front**. Windowless on the return, which is what a party wall is, but the same material. **[One number I am NOT quoting]** my world-wide sweep also reported "143 flat-colour elevations". That figure is junk: the filter takes whole building MESHES, so a solid block box counts as one "elevation" and its roof tone reads as a flat face. It is not evidence of anything and must not be cited — the `#53382e` count and the three walked comparisons are what this confirmation rests on. scripts/flanks.mjs, bankflank.mjs, bankside.mjs.

## [G] casino interior is nice but i want more. bigger and more

— **AUDITOR CONFIRMED on the ask; the coherence problem I raised mid-flight is UNRESOLVED and has grown (build 7a2c9befc).** **Delivered on its own terms:** the casino is **396 m², 81% free floor, median passage 6.25 m — second-most open of the ten rooms**, behind only the hotel. "Bigger and more expansive" is plainly done. **Still true, and worse:** the interior is larger than the building it sits in. The GOLDEN ACES street door stands on a collider measuring **11.55 × 14.30 = 165 m²** — the same exterior figure I measured mid-flight, so the building has not moved — while the interior is now **396 m²**. That is **2.40×**, against the **1.96×** I flagged before it landed. **Not a rejection of this row:** the user asked for a bigger casino and got one, and nothing a player does exposes the discrepancy directly. It is a set-level fact the desk should hold, because it only gets harder to reconcile as the room grows. **[Stated limit]** I can give this ratio for the casino because a single collider carries its door. I **cannot** give it for the library — no large collider contains the library door point, so my finder returned an 8 × 5 = 40 m² porch, and 440 against 40 would be a nonsense I would have published if I had not looked at it.

— **AUDITOR, REPUBLISHING: every interior coordinate in this cell predates the BANK (build 92db51e7f).** A `bank` room has since been inserted at cx 440 and **all ten original rooms moved +80 m** — bodega 440→520, burger 520→600, casino 600→680, church 680→760, diner 760→840, hotel 840→920, library 920→1000, pawn 1000→1080, tax 1080→1160, thrift 1160→1240. **The measurements above are correct for the build they were taken on and their x values now name different rooms** — anything I called "the casino at 600" is the burger today. **Ask `roomDims()` for the `id`; never infer a room from its x.** `notes/room-shift.md`.

## [G] hotel exterior looks nice / interior doesnt match the ex

— **AUDITOR CONFIRMED that the interior reads as the hotel's own palette; ONE PART OF THE CLAIM IS NOT ACCURATE, and the reason matters (build 3238fe7a8).** **Delivered:** the lobby reads unmistakably as a red-and-gold hotel — 44.1% of the rendered frame is warm and 42.0% strongly red, against a street exterior that is 0.0% strongly red. Patterned carpet, gold picture rail, brass behind the desk. **The constants are real:** `vice.ts:184` declares `GOLD #d8a83a, GOLD_D #8a6a22, RED #8e1f2a, RED_D #5a1520`. **Two of the three named are used verbatim inside** — `#5a1520` and `#8a6a22` both appear in `int-hotel.ts` as literals. **But `#8e1f2a` — RED itself, the main one — appears in `int-hotel.ts` ONLY IN THE COMMENT that names it.** The wall is `0x6d2029` and the floor `0x5a2430`: close to RED, and **lookalikes are exactly what the row says these are not**. **And none of them is imported.** `vice.ts:184` declares them inside a function scope, so they cannot be, and both files carry duplicate literals. **The values agree today and nothing keeps them agreeing** — change the exterior and the interior silently does not follow. Same shape as the four hand-copied `minFilter` calls H fixed by moving the rule up a level; the fix here is to export the palette from `vice.ts` and import it. Not a fault in what the user sees, and a real fragility in how it is held together.
